Employment growth of around 0.5% would be more than
enough to absorb the modest 0.3% rise in the working-
age population, and on its own would suggest a fall in

the unemployment rate. But it also comes down to the
rate of labour force participation, which we don’t really
have much guidance on. The participation rate has been
choppy over recent quarters, and in the June quarter
we saw an unusually large 0.3pt rise to 70.7%; we’re
assuming some payback this time with a more modest
0.1pt rise to 70.8%.
We’ll also be keeping an eye on the broader indicators of
slack in the labour market, although we don’t formally use

these in our forecasts. The June quarter saw a sharp rise
in the underutilisation rate from 12.9% to 13.8%. However,
we think there’s some residual seasonality in this series,
relating to one component in particular: people who are
available for work but not actively looking. In that case,
we could see a substantial pullback in the underutilisation
measure this time.
Previously we’ve characterised the labour market as a
“low-fire, low-hire” environment, with much of the rise in

unemployment reflecting the difficulty of breaking into
the workforce, rather than job losses. There are some
signs of a thaw emerging, with job vacancies picking up
from their lows over the last year and business surveys
pointing to a lift in hiring intentions. But we still expect
that it will be a long road to recovery – our current
forecasts see the unemployment rate at 5% by the end of
next year.
While cost-of-living pressures are again rearing their

head as a major concern for workers, the existing degree
of slack in the labour market will continue to make it
difficult for them to push for bigger pay increases. We
expect a 0.5% rise in the Labour Cost Index (LCI) for
the September quarter, which would nudge the annual
growth rate up slightly to 2.1%. That said, we don’t have a
lot to go on for this forecast. The MEI includes a measure
of median weekly earnings, which has shown no sign of
acceleration so far – but this is only a very loose indicator

of wage pressures, as it will also reflect any changes in
the composition of jobs and pay rates.

Charts of the week: Watching earnings and a potential ‘Blue wave’

Editorial 
Policy, politics and earnings  
Strategy summary:  We retain our near -term defensive and 
diversified stance across assets, sectors and regions amid 
near-term uncertainty around monetary policy, politics and 
earnings. The next two weeks will be busy for major central 
banks, US political watchers and technology leaders powering 
the AI supercycle. We expect the Fed, ECB and BoJ to hold 
rates next week, but markets will focus on whether central 
banks are turning more hawkish due to the latest surge in fuel 
prices. The US mid -term elections are expected to give 
Democrats control of the House and Senate, with implications 
for taxes and the AI-led investment boom.  
Meanwhile, the consensus is expecting the US tech sector to 
report over 60% y/y earnings growth for Q3, the biggest jump 
in over two decades outside the post-COVID rebound.  Each of 
these three drivers have the potential to surprise markets.  
Big Three to hold monetary policy: We expect the Fed, ECB 
and BoJ to hold policy rates next week, after September’s 
hikes, as they assess whether higher bond yields have 
tightened financial conditions enough to offset fuel-price-driven 
inflation. We expect the Big Three to hike next in December as 
fuel prices are likely to stay elevated into early next year due to 
refining capacity constraints. Nevertheless, the Fed and ECB 
are likely to deliver less than around 60bps of rate hikes priced 
in by money markets over the next 12 months on the back of 
an eventual easing of oil and fuel prices leading to disinflation 
by H2 2027. This outlook means 3 -7-year Developed Market 
investment grade bonds currently offer attractive risk-reward.  
Opportunity in Australian bonds. Other major central banks, 
such as the Bank of England, Bank of Canada and Reserve 
Bank of New Zealand are likely to hike rates by 25bps in the 
next two weeks given their focus on taming near-term inflation. 
However, we believe the Reserve Bank of Australia (RBA) is 
close to the end of its hiking cycle, even if it hikes one last time 
in the coming months (money markets give a 60% chance of a 
hike on 3 November). Australia’s domestic labour and housing 
markets and business confidence are softening after the RBA 
hiked its policy rate four times this year to a 15 -year high. 
Meanwhile, Australia’s cash rate is 60bps higher than the US 
policy rate, supporting the AUD. We continue to see attractive 
income and FX gain opportunities from AUD corporate bonds.  
A ‘Blue Wave’ in the US?  Prediction markets strongly favour 
Democrats taking the House of Representatives and possibly 
the Senate in the 3 November US mid-term elections. While an 
opposition sweep in the mid-terms has historically delivered the 
best 12-month returns for equities  among all sc enarios, partly 
due to the resulting policy gridlock, a decisive Democratic “blue 
wave” could increase longer -term risks of higher taxes and 
uncertainty over policy towards AI investment and data centres. 
With the Senate race still close and prediction markets fa llible, 
we remain cautious on risk assets in the near term.  
Closing EU bank contingent convertible bond s (CoCos1) 
and US 10 -year go vernment bond ideas:  We lock in 2% 
gains in EU CoCos and 1.7% gain in US 10 -year government 
bond ideas amid French and US political risks (page 5-6). We 
continue to hedge any inflation risks through inflation-protected 
bonds and geopolitical risk through an Overweight in gold.  
Watching Big Tech earnings:   Upcoming Big Tech earnings 
raises uncertainty as market leadership remains concentrated 
and share -price reactions more mixed. With valuations less 
likely to drive returns, earnings delivery and sustainability of AI 
investment, funding and monet isation and broadening out of 
earnings beyond tech will be critical, raising the risk of volatility 
and sector divergence.  We remain invested but defensively 
positioned, favouring diversification across sectors and regions.  
Staying defensive and diversified. We recently upgraded the 
defensive US Healthcare sector to Overweight , while retaining 
our Overweights to US tech and materials as well as financials 
in the US, Euro area and Japan. We also downgraded the more 
volatile Communications Services sector to Neutral  and took 
profits on the semiconductor segment earlier this month before 
the latest correction. Our defensive and diversified stance 
should preserve participation in further equity upside, while 
reducing exposure to earnings disappointments and supporting 
resilience through the reporting season.  
—  Rajat Bhattacharya

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 23 October 2026

Top client questions 
  What are the key takeaways from the technology sector 
earnings reported so far this week? 
Our view: Recent  technology sector earnings results point to 
strong compute demand and continued capex expansion, 
supporting our positive long -term view on the AI investment 
theme. However, rising funding and execution risks warrant 
near-term caution. We prefer to avoid increasing exposure to 
higher-risk areas within the technology sector for now. 
Rationale: Earnings results reported this week suggest major tech 
companies remain committed to expanding their AI infrastructure. 
While this bodes well for AI infrastructure providers, ambitious 
expansion plans imply that an increasing share of AI capex is likely 
to depend on debt financing. We expect debt-financed AI capex to 
rise from c.25% of AI capex in 2026 to c.40% in 2027.  
Greater reliance on borrowing increases sensitivity to interest rates, 
while deployment delays could postpone revenue recognition amid 
ongoing financing costs. However, evidence of improving AI 
monetisation and adoption should strengthen operating cash 
flows and support internal capex funding. Against this backdrop, 
we will assess Big Tech earnings next week for greater clarity on 
capex guidance and funding and AI monetisation trends.  
—  Ryan Goh, Investment Strategist 
  Are you still positive on Euro  area bank equities amid 
heightened political and fiscal uncertainty in the region? 
Our view: We remain positive on Euro  area bank equities, 
supported by resilient earnings and shareholder-friendly capital 
return policies. While Fr ance’s fiscal uncertainty and a softer 
growth outlook warrant caution, the recent pullback in Euro  
area bank equities  offers selective opportunities. We favour 
well-capitalised banks with strong deposit franchises and 
diversified earnings. 
Rationale: We expect Euro  area banks’ Q3 2026 net interest 
income to grow 1.5 -3.0% q/q , led by Spanish banks, driven by 
higher rates and continued asset repricing. Resilient fee income from 
wealth management and investment banking activity, as well as 
dividends and buybacks, should provide further support. Price-to-
book (P/B) valuations have eased , despite expectations for 
healthy return on equity (ROE), thus improving risk-reward. 
Fiscal uncertainty in France has widened sovereign spreads, 
pressuring banks’ bond holdings and funding costs . Although 
French banks represent 13% of the Euro  area Bank Index, volatility 
could spread to regional financial markets. However, stress in the 
broader banking system appears limited , supported by strong 
capital buffers and ECB tools to contain disorderly market moves and 
sovereign market contagion. We will continue to monitor credit 
trends, sovereign spreads and banks’ 2027 earnings guidance. 
—  Robin Xie, Senior Investment Strategist

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 23 October 2026

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  What has driven the recent weakness in  China equities, 
and what is your outlook from here? 
Our view: We maintain China equities as  a Core holding , as 
valuations remain inexpensive. Rapid technological innovation 
and investment in  AI localisation provide tailwinds, but the 
potential for a re-rating appears delayed. Investors should seek 
to diversify their portfolios and reduce concentration risk. 
Rationale: China equities have weak ened, driven by mounting 
scepticism over AI capex returns, structural margin 
compression and concerns about the country’s significant 
financing requirements. Another notable feature in China has been 
significant share issuance, which has diluted earnings per share and 
weighed on the MSCI China Index’s performance. These pressures 
have been compounded by geopolitical headwinds, restrictions on 
advanced chips, an enduring property sect or contraction and weak 
domestic demand. September’s stimulus measures also 
disappointed markets in terms of their scale and pace of delivery. 
Notwithstanding these headwinds, valuations remain inexpensive, 
supported by rapid domestic innovation and structural tailwinds 
in AI localisation , leading us to retain China equities as a Core 
holding. Elevated volatility reinforces the need to be defensively 
positioned, favouring diversification rather than relying on technology 
sector concentration. We also remain Overweight China’s 
healthcare, financial and materials sectors. 
—  Cindy Lam, CFA, Senior Investment Strategist

Are you still bullish on US 10-year government bonds?   
Our view: We are closing our opportunistic bullish idea on US 
10-year government bonds at a gain of 1.7%. The risk -reward 
has become less compelling after the yield broke below 5%, the 
lower bound of our three-month forecast range of 5.00-5.25%. 
Rationale: The US 10 -year yield fell  over 17bps to 4.97% on 22 
October – its first move below 5% since 23 September and biggest 
one-day drop since March 2023. A sell-off in AI-related equities and 
renewed Middle East tensions drove investors towards safe havens. 
Earlier this month, the yield hit its highest level in over two decades. 
The forces behind the rally are fragile, though.  Oil prices remain 
elevated amid escalating Middle East tensions, and September US 
consumer inflation rose to 3.7% y/y . Fed officials  have signalled 
there is no urgency to raise rates at the 27-28 October meeting, 
but persistent price pressures keep further tightening on the table. 
We prefer to lock in gains and close our opportunistic bullish idea on 
10-year government bonds at a gain of 1.7%. 
—  Jonathan Liang, CFA, CIO, Fixed Income & FX

### Page 6

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 23 October 2026

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  What is your outlook for French bank bonds , especially 
ahead of France’s upcoming sovereign credit rating reviews?   
Our view: The recent sell-off in French bank bonds is a repricing 
of tail risks, not a credit story. We expect further price volatility 
until the April 2027 French presidential election, particularly at 
the junior end of the capital structure, so we are closing our 
opportunistic EU bank contingent convertibles (CoCos1) idea at 
a gain of 2.0%. 
Rationale: We believe the recent sell -off in French bank bonds 
reflects domestic political and fiscal risks rather than weaker 
credit fundamentals . The sustained widening of the OAT -Bund 
spread has raised concerns about the profit and loss (P&L) impact 
on banks' sovereign debt holdings. Banks provide limited 
disclosure on these exposures. However, banks’ investment 
portfolios are generally diversified, and holdings booked at amortised 
cost should soften the P&L impact. Near -term catalysts include the 
upcoming sovereign credit rating rev iews. A sovereign downgrade 
could trigger bank rating downgrades, with domestically focused 
lenders potentially more exposed than regionally diversified peers. A 
sovereign credit event is not our base case, but we expect headline 
risks to persist until the  French presidential election . Against this 
backdrop, we are closing our opportunistic EU bank CoCos idea. 
—  Cedric Lam, Senior Investment Strategist 
  Will the November US mid-term elections and European 
bond volatility support a sustained Swiss franc (CHF) rebound?  
Our view: We expect a near -term CHF rebound, supported 
mainly by safe -haven demand amid European bond volatility. 
We believe the US mid-term elections will be a secondary driver. 
Rationale: European sovereign bond volatility provides the stronger 
catalyst for CHF strength. The Fr ance-Germany government bond 
yield spread remains elevated near 160bps, reflecting heightened 
French fiscal concerns and reinforcing the CHF’s safe-haven appeal. 
The US mid-terms could provide additional support for the CHF. 
A Democratic victory in either chamber is expected to  constrain 
further fiscal expansion, potentially weighing on the USD. However, 
this would not necessarily lead to faster fiscal consolidation. 
The SNB’s 0% policy rate leaves  the CHF at a sizeable interest -
rate disadvantage vs. the USD, constraining sustained CHF gains. 
With EUR/CHF below 0.90 , excessive CHF strength could also 
increase the risk of SNB intervention . A decisive USD/CHF break 
below 0.8130 would strengthen the technical case for a more 
sustained CHF rebound.  
—  Vincent Tan, Senior Investment Strategist 
—  Iris Yuen, Investment Strategist

A widening France-Germany 10-year government

(IG) bond option-adjusted spread (OAS) suggest a 
repricing of political and fiscal risk rather than 
credit risk

Standard Chartered Bank

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  Would worsening budget deficits and political uncertainty 
across parts of Europe affect your view on EUR/USD and Euro 
area government bond yields? 
Our view: Fiscal and political concerns constrain near -term 
EUR/USD gains, with France being the primary vulnerability. We 
expect EUR/USD to recover towards the 1.17-1.18 range over 
the next  12 months, supported by continued Euro area 
economic growth and an expected fading of USD support. We 
expect wider differentials in Euro area government bond yields, 
with political risk premium elevated in Fr ench government 
bonds (OATs) and German government bonds (Bunds) 
supported by safe-haven demand. 
Rationale: France's high  public debt, large budget deficit and 
political obstacles to fiscal adjustment s are  weighing on market 
confidence, with the country’s  10-year government bond yield 
rising above 5%. The risk of stress spreading to Italy and Spain 
adds to regional concerns, although current evidence does not 
establish a broad European fiscal crisis. German government bonds 
should benefit from safe-haven demand, while fiscal concerns keep 
risk premi a on vulnerable countries' bonds elevated. EUR/USD 
remains more sensitive to US-Germany interest-rate differentials 
and broader market confidence than to French bond yields alone. 
France’s fiscal risks, e levated energy costs and high US interest 
rates weigh on the EUR in the near term. Over the next 12 months, 
Germany’s multi -year public investment programme, resilient 
exports and sound banking-sector fundamentals are expected to 
support Euro area growth. We expect the Fed's rate-hiking cycle to 
peak by mid -2027, reducing USD support and driving  the EUR  
towards 1.17-1.18 . 
The principal tail risk  is fiscal stress widening government bond 
yield spreads and spilling over into bank funding  conditions and 
lending activity, weakening growth and delaying the EUR's recovery. 
—  Vincent Tan, Senior Investment Strategist 
—  Cedric Lam, Senior Investment Strategist

Document 425: The FOMC pause to reflect (2026-11-05)

Source PDF: all report pdf/The FOMC pause to reflect (2026-11-05).pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 10
Status: ok

### Page 1

#fomc #usd #us
The FOMC pause to reflect
By Elliot Clarke
Head of International Economics, Westpac
08:14 October 29 2026 Share
No change in October, though confidence in the economy's strength and two dissents point
to a hike in December.
The FOMC held at their October meeting, albeit with two dissents, Governor Cook and Cleveland Fed
President Hammack preferring to hike by 25bps at this meeting. The statement was short and, in the
press conference, Chair Warsh again declined to provide definitive guidance on the near-term policy

### Page 2

Related articles
outlook.
The Committee remains positive on economic activity, characterising it in the statement as “expanding
at a strong pace” (an upgrade from “solid” in September). Despite elevated uncertainty, “domestic
spending has been resilient”, underpinned by “strong” productivity growth and “robust” capital
investment. Chair Warsh subsequently also emphasised the breadth of economic growth outside of
tech-related investment. Labour demand and supply were represented as balanced and resilient in

both the statement and press conference.
The Committee refrained from spotlighting key price risks in the statement. However, inflation “remains
elevated” and the FOMC are determined to achieve a “timely” and sustainable return of annual
consumer inflation to 2.0%yr after 6 years above target.
Along with the two dissents, this guidance and the “accumulation of more information will help the
Committee determine the policy stance” suggests another 25bp hike is likely in December. Thereafter,

the FOMC will remain attentive to any broadening in inflation and related risks through 2027.
Towards the end of the press conference, Chair Warsh also announced a structural change in the
FOMC schedule. While there will still be 8 meetings in 2027, a meeting in the fall will focus on
monetary policy strategy versus the current stance of monetary policy. Chair Warsh then intends for 2
of the 8 meetings in 2028 to have this strategy focus. If realised, this will see the FOMC review and

vote on the current stance of monetary policy just 6 times per calendar year from 2028.

Document 471: Why hasn’t there been a major financial crisis in recent years_ (2026-11-03)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Why hasn’t there been a major financial crisis in recent years_ (2026-11-03).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-11-03
Pages: 19
Status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

Why hasn’t there been a major 
financial crisis in recent years?

1 
Authors 
Henry Allen 
Macro Strategist

Peter Sidorov, CFA 
Senior Economist

Camilla Siazon 
Research Analyst

November 3, 2026 
— The economist Hyman Minsky warned, in essence, that “stability is destabilising” . He 
argued that a lengthy period of financial stability could be problematic, as it induced 
risk-taking and complacency amongst investors, laying the foundation for the next 
crisis. For example, lenders relax their terms and borrowers are more willing to extend 
themselves, as nobody wants to miss out. This dynamic makes economies inherently 
prone to financial panics and boom-bust cycles. 
— But since the 2008 crisis, and the subsequent sovereign crisis in the Eurozone, we 
have yet to see another financial crisis happen on a scale that had a lasting market 
and macroeconomic impact. There have been clear moments of concern, as in early 
2023 with the regional bank turmoil, but nothing that has caused a lasting impact. So 
this led us to ask why. 
— In this paper, we examine why the post-GFC era has seen fewer financial crises. 
Explanations include the reforms made after 2008 to avoid a repeat, the lower 
proportion of financial assets held by the banking system, as well as the speedier 
reaction of policymakers when wobbles have occurred. We then consider a few 
candidates that could cause the next crisis, including AI vulnerabilities, private credit, 
and stretched fiscal positions. 
— Ultimately, financial crises haven’t been abolished, but the reforms of recent years 
have made it harder for shocks to propagate through the traditional banking system. 
Moreover, policymakers have become far more inclined to intervene when initial signs 
of stress have emerged.

### Page 2

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 2 Deutsche Bank AG
Section 1: What is a financial crisis, and what causes them?
There isn’t a single technical definition of a “financial crisis”, and they come in many
different types. Commonly, we think of banking crises like 2008, but they can also
encompass currency/balance of payments crises, debt crises and other forms too.
Given currency and sovereign crises have been more common in emerging markets
(the UK's ERM crisis in 1992 and the euro crisis being the two most notable DM

occurrences), historical analysis in developed economies has tended to focus
heavily on banking crises. Non-bank funding or market-liquidity crises are often not
categorised separately in the literature, but they have become more common in
recent years. This includes the "dash-for-cash" stress at the onset of the Covid
pandemic in March 2020 and the 2022 LDI crisis in the UK.
But for all the different types of financial crisis, there are common themes between

them. Often, they are accompanied by asset bubbles that burst in a non-linear
fashion. We generally see a severe tightening in credit conditions, which in turn
flows through into a broader economic contraction. Frequently, there are
contagion-like dynamics that can be difficult to stop. And in the modern world, they
usually require policymakers to step in, stabilising the situation with interventions.
At risk of oversimplification, we note four factors that determine the emergence and

the severity of a financial crisis. The first three are well-established in the financial
literature, sometimes pooled together into a build-up and a crisis phase
(Brunnermeier & Oehmke 2013), while the fourth has become increasingly
important over the past 15-20 years:
1.  Underlying imbalances. Bubbles and financial imbalances build during
the run-up phase, leaving accumulated risks that materialise when the
crisis erupts. This is the build-up typically described by the early phases of

Kindleberger's model of financial crises.
2.  Trigger. This is the shock that turns an existing vulnerability into clear
financial stress. These are often very difficult to predict and we mostly leave
these outside the focus of this report.
3.  Amplification and financial system fragility. Various factors determine
whether a shock propagates through the financial system, and the
intensity of that shock. These include leverage, liquidity and maturity

mismatches, interconnectedness, and the concentration of exposures.
Other channels in the financial system can then amplify the shock,
including bank runs, fire sales and margin calls, and negative net worth
effects, sovereign–bank loops etc.
4.  Intervention/policy response. This has been increasingly important in
recent decades as the size and scope of policy response to crises has
increased. The outright size can vary, and intervention can occur across

several dimensions, ranging from direct intervention in financial markets,
to counter-cyclical macroeconomic policy that mitigates the impact on the
real economy.
Clearly, these factors are not independent and the interaction between them is what
determines the severity of a financial crisis.

### Page 3

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 3
One important aspect is the joint role that underlying imbalances and amplification
play. While theoretical models such as Diamond & Dybvig highlight the potential for
a bank run that triggers a broad crisis, empirical research shows that in practice, it
is exceedingly rare for a bank run to lead to bank failure in the absence of an
underlying solvency issue (Correia, Luck and Verner 2026). In other words, the

presence of amplification channels alone does not lead to a crisis.
Conversely, in the absence of amplification, it is possible to get significant
turbulence in asset prices, but without a financial crisis. For instance, a bear market,
wider credit spreads, or even a recession do not in and of themselves imply there
is a financial crisis. This was seen clearly with the bursting of the dot com bubble,
a severe equity decline but not a financial crisis, though it did cause a recession. At

the same time, in the chart below, we can see several US equity bear markets that
took place outside of recessions. On other occasions, however, a “Minsky
Moment” can then happen, where even a seemingly minor event can trigger a high-
profile incident that cascades across the financial system.
Figure 1: S&P 500 drawdowns over time, with shaded recessions showing select episodes when the index fell over 20%
without coinciding with a recession
Source :Bloomberg Finance LP, NBER, Deutsche Bank Research

Looking back through history, we can see how variation in these factors has led to
different regimes over time in terms of frequency/severity of financial factors.

### Page 4

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 4 Deutsche Bank AG
Section 2. Why have financial crises been more or less likely
in certain periods of history?
1. Pre-WWII: More frequent financial instability, albeit under a very
different system.
Financial instability was a recurrent feature of the pre-WWII era. During the 19th
and early 20th centuries, there were frequent panics that often led to major slumps.
For instance, the Panic of 1873 sparked a period often known as the “Long

Depression”, which extended into the 1890s for many countries.
In part, financial panics occurred more frequently because policy operated under
very different settings. For instance, the Gold Standard as the monetary system
constrained a countercyclical policy response and transmitted deflation
internationally, while falling prices increased real debt burdens. Moreover, the
Federal Reserve wasn’t established until 1913, and the lack of an effective lender of

last resort meant there was a private intervention during the US Panic of 1907. The
first wave of globalisation also brought new vulnerabilities. Between 1870 and
1913, many emerging economies accumulated debts denominated in gold or
foreign currency, leaving them exposed when capital inflows reversed or exchange
rates weakened.
The biggest and most famous crisis occurred in 1929, when the Wall Street Crash
of that year sparked the Great Depression. The length and depth of that crisis helped

to stimulate the study of modern macroeconomics, with huge efforts to prevent a
repeat over the years ahead. Indeed, the failures of the response to the Great
Depression played a large role in the much swifter response to the 2008 financial
crisis, helping to avoid a second Great Depression.
That experience established much of the modern financial safety net that exists to
this day. For instance in the US, the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation was

established in 1933. The US and many other countries came off the Gold Standard
as well in this period. There were also regulatory reforms, and the Glass-Steagall Act
separated out commercial from investment banking.
2. 1944-1971: The Bretton Woods era coincided with fewer financial
crises
Around the end of WWII, economic policymakers sought to create an international
monetary system for the post-war era. The relative financial calm that followed

reflected a combination of restrictions on international capital movement, tightly
regulated domestic financial systems, and a system of fixed exchange rates that
aimed to provide stability and prevent the competitive devaluations that occurred
in the 1930s. This saw exchange rates pegged against the US Dollar, which in turn
was convertible to gold at $35 per ounce.
At the same time, capital controls helped preserve monetary policy flexibility, while

maintaining the system of fixed exchange rates. These controls had several
downsides but they also insulated countries from “sudden stop” crises that can
occur, when capital rapidly flees a country. Moreover, capital controls prevented
the rapid buildup of cross-border systemic leverage. So it was more difficult to
generate a global financial crisis in the same way.

### Page 5

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 5
Figure 2: Gold price (USD/oz, log scale)
10

In the Bretton Woods
era, the dollar was tied
to gold
Source : Finaeon, Deutsche Bank
Alongside capital controls, there were also domestic reforms in the aftermath of the
Great Depression, which sought to avoid a repeat. As mentioned earlier, the US
Glass-Steagall Act of 1933 separated commercial banking from investment

banking. Another example of the era’s regulated financial system in the US was
Regulation Q, which imposed maximum interest rates that commercial banks
could pay on deposits. That meant depositors could not search for yield and cause
a run on deposits as easily. Another rationale was that with institutions not
competing for deposits, it would avoid a compression in the margin between the
rates banks paid on their lending and borrowing, which would disincentivise a push

into riskier investments.
Finally, economic growth was also stronger in this period, with the period between
WWII and the 1973 oil shock often described as the “Golden Age”. This played a role
in avoiding financial crises too, as faster growth helped keep debt default rates
down, avoiding the kind of “trigger” events above that might spark a financial crisis.
Coupled with financial repression, strong growth also helped to bring down the
high post-war public debt burdens.

Over time however, the peg between the dollar and gold found itself under growing
strain. The economist Robert Triffin identified the “Triffin dilemma”. Supplying the
world with dollar reserves resulted in outstanding dollar liabilities around the world
that exceeded the gold reserves in the US, weakening confidence that dollars could
ultimately be redeemed for gold. Conversely, restricting dollar supply to protect
those reserves and preserve convertibility risked depriving the system of the

liquidity needed to support trade and growth.
These tensions intensified in the late 1960s amid expansionary US policies,
including spending on the Vietnam War and Great Society programmes. The
continued drain in US gold reserves eventually led the US to suspend the
convertibility of the US Dollar into gold, effectively ending the Bretton Woods
system. Initially, there were attempts to bring some kind of fixed-exchange rate
system back, but the world of fiat currencies (with no link to a precious metal)

persists to this day.

### Page 6

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 6 Deutsche Bank AG
3. 1971-2008: Financial crises become more frequent after the
breakdown of Bretton Woods to the Global Financial Crisis
After the breakdown of the Bretton Woods system, financial crises became a more
regular occurrence. While the end of the gold convertibility did not by itself trigger
future crises, it opened the door for wider financial liberalisation, with the resulting
rise in capital mobility and looser domestic financial regulation then helping to

facilitate a global build-up of imbalances.
The 1980s and 1990s were a period of frequent crisis events. The transition away
from capital controls meant that capital could flow more freely across borders.
However, this could also cause a “sudden stop” if investors suddenly pulled their
capital out of a country, contributing to bank runs and currency collapses.
Moreover, many emerging markets borrowed in foreign currencies like the US
Dollar, meaning that a depreciation in their own currency would see this USD-

denominated debt surge in local currency terms. This meant it was a particular
problem if US interest rates were increasing, as it became harder for many
emerging markets to repay their debts. This was a contributing factor behind the
Latin American debt crisis in the early 1980s. And the rate hikes also had domestic
ramifications, being a contributing factor behind the Savings and Loan crisis in the
United States.
With capital flowing more freely, that also played a role in the 1994 Mexican Peso

Crisis, and the 1997 Asian Financial Crisis. A sudden reversal in capital flows led to
crisis and ultimately recession in many countries. The following year, the Russian
financial crisis then took place, and fears grew in the United States about the wider
ramifications, as the hedge fund Long-Term Capital Management (LTCM) came
under pressure. Indeed, this period saw 75bps of pre-emptive rate cuts from the
Federal Reserve.
This volatility also saw the Fed proactively seek to step in and smooth out the

economic volatility. This was broadly successful, but in the process also helped
engender complacency and a further buildup of leverage. Indeed, after the dot com
bubble burst, the federal funds rate was cut to just 1% in mid-2003, where it
remained until mid-2004.
This period saw clear optimism that another Great Depression was highly unlikely.
For instance, Nobel Prize-winning economist Robert Lucas argued in his 2003 AEA
Presidential Address that “macroeconomics in this original sense has succeeded:

Its central problem of depression prevention has been solved, for all practical
purposes, and has in fact been solved for many decades.”
However, as we now know, the 2008 financial crisis occurred, driving the biggest
global downturn since the Great Depression of the 1930s. It turned out that Minsky
had been correct, and a lengthy period without a crisis had once again helped to
induce complacency, laying the foundations for a repeat.

Section 3: The Post-2008 world: Why have we not had
another big financial crisis?
Since the Global Financial Crisis of 2008, we have thankfully avoided a major
financial crisis. Despite clear moments of stress, most notably around the collapse
of SVB in March 2023, we have not seen a similar wave of bank collapses on that
scale. There are several components to this.
First, post-GFC deleveraging cleared out risks in the system, with countries and
sectors that had excess indebtedness before the GFC experiencing pronounced

declines in leverage.
Second, there have been reforms explicitly designed to avoid a repeat of 2008.
After the crisis happened, there was a clear recognition that gaps in regulation had
exacerbated the crisis, leading to effects that cascaded across the financial system.
For instance, the Basel III reforms required banks to hold more equity, with a
minimum requirement for Common Equity Tier 1 (CET1) capital. They also
introduced the liquidity coverage ratio, which requires banks to hold enough high-

quality liquid assets to ensure they can cover their net cash outflows under a 30-day
stressed scenario. In addition, the net stable funding ratio was brought in, requiring
banks to have sufficient stable funding that exceeded the required amount of stable
funding for a one-year stressed scenario.
At the national level, further reforms were also introduced. In the US, the Dodd-
Frank Act was introduced, with measures including the Volcker Rule that restricted

banks from engaging in proprietary trading, and required the Federal Reserve to
perform an annual stress test of large institutions. Meanwhile in the European
Union, which also experienced a sovereign debt crisis, reforms included a “Single
Supervisory Mechanism”, whereby banking supervision was brought under the
ECB, ensuring harmonised regulatory standards.

### Page 8

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 8 Deutsche Bank AG
Third, the share of financial assets held by banks has fallen since the 2008 crisis.
This hasn’t eliminated risk, but commercial banks are funded by retail deposits that
can quickly fly. By contrast, non-bank financial institutions (NBFIs), e.g. hedge
funds, are not as prone to these bank run dynamics. For instance, a private credit
closed-ended fund is not one where investors can simply redeem their funds on

demand.
We can see this shift in the data. Looking at the FSB’s group of 21 plus the Eurozone,
we can see that the share of assets held by banks has fallen from a peak of 46% in
2008, to 38% in 2024. By contrast, the share held by NBFIs has risen from 43% to
51%.
Figure 5: Share of Assets of Financial Corporations (FSB 21 + EA Group)
35%

Banks Nonbank Financial Intermediation Sector (NBFI)
Source :Haver Analytics, Deutsche Bank

Fourth, the lessons of 2008 have meant that when the financial system has had
wobbles since, policymakers have acted with incredible speed to stop the
turmoil.
This was most obvious when the Covid-19 pandemic began. Whilst a pandemic
was very different to a financial crisis, there was initially huge turmoil in financial
markets. Indeed, the S&P 500 fell by more than a third in just over a month, a decline
at a speed and scale unseen since the Great Depression. Meanwhile, US HY credit

spreads soared to levels last seen around the financial crisis, and at the height of the
turmoil, there was a huge spike in real yields, despite the economy entering a
recession.
Against this backdrop, central banks wasted no time in acting. For instance, the Fed
delivered an emergency 50bp rate cut, followed by another 100bp rate cut.
Moreover, they swiftly resumed Quantitative Easing, and announced the
establishment of multiple new facilities to ensure the smooth functioning of credit

markets. These included a Primary Market Corporate Credit Facility, to support new
bond and loan issuance, along with a Secondary Market Corporate Credit facility,
which offered liquidity for outstanding corporate bonds. Meanwhile, a third facility,
called the Term Asset-Backed Securities Loan Facility, was set up to enable the
issuance of asset-backed securities. Similarly in Europe, the ECB set up a new
Pandemic Emergency Purchase Programme (PEPP), which ultimately had a total

envelope of €1.85tn.
Whilst the pandemic caused a huge economic shock, the swift action meant it did
not translate into a financial one, and the brief tightening in credit conditions was

### Page 9

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 9
unwound over the coming months.
A similar pattern was seen during the regional banking turmoil of March 2023. This
was a financial panic, which initially began with the collapse of Silicon Valley Bank.
However, this began to spread, and Signature Bank also failed. Then in Europe,
Credit Suisse came under investor scrutiny and saw large deposit outflows, which
culminated in a purchase by UBS that included guarantees from the Swiss

government. This coincided with huge market moves, with the 2yr Treasury yield
posting its biggest daily decline in over 40 years, with a 61bps decline on March 13.
However, policymakers swiftly moved to stop any contagion. The US Treasury,
Federal Reserve and the FDIC released a joint statement stating that all deposits at
SVB would be protected, and that the Federal Reserve would provide additional
funding to ensure banks could meet the needs of depositors. Meanwhile, the Fed’s

balance sheet briefly expanded again, despite the fact it had begun the process of
quantitative tightening. These actions ultimately meant the crisis proved contained
and did not lead to a wave of further bank collapses in the manner of 2008.

Fifth, policymakers have also responded to recent shocks with huge fiscal

transfers, meaning that debt has migrated from private towards sovereign
balance sheets.
Whilst global debt has risen slightly over the last decade, if you dig deeper into the
numbers, that’s entirely driven by a rise in government debt. If we compare the
statistics for Q2 2026, relative to Q2 2016, there’s actually been a decline in debt for
households, financial corporations, and nonfinancial corporations. The only one of
the four segments that’s gone up in the last decade is government debt.

In relative terms, the transformation is even bigger if we look back to 2007, before
the Global Financial Crisis kicked off. Back then, government debt was the smallest
of the four categories, but because of the GFC and the pandemic, it’s now become
the biggest of the four categories.
That in turn has led to separate issues, with government bond yields at multi-year
highs, alongside rising net interest spending. So the fiscal space has become
limited. However, public balance sheets don’t face the same kind of system-wide

run that a bank would.

Overall, the post-2008 experience offers three key forward-looking lessons. First,
stronger bank balance sheets and the shift towards non-bank finance suggest

future stress may originate outside banks. As such, historical analyses centred on
banking crises may provide a less complete guide. Second, policymakers have
become increasingly effective at containing liquidity pressures and amplification.
And third, the shift in debt towards sovereign balance sheets has cushioned private-
sector shocks but narrowed the capacity for future intervention.
Section 4: What are potential candidates for the next crisis?

This section considers some of the current risks in the financial system and whether
they could be sowing the seeds for the next financial crisis. This section is not
designed to be a comprehensive list of financial risks, but rather an assessment of
three important areas where risks have risen in recent years.
1. Could AI spark broader vulnerabilities?
A huge driver of the current equity bull run has been the growth in AI-related stocks.
This report won’t explore the debate as to whether this is a bubble, but examine

whether a correction could eventually propagate into a systemic financial crisis. As
noted earlier, a reversal in the investment cycle need not cause a crisis in itself, and
the bursting of the dot com bubble also did not lead to a broader financial crisis.
Nevertheless, there are issues that could spark vulnerabilities. First, the rapid rise
in AI capex raises questions about whether the investment will pay off. The AI build-
out is on course to exceed the scale of past technological investment cycles, which

have often involved overinvestment even when the technologies ultimately proved
transformative. Second, funding is shifting from predominantly operating cash
flow towards external capital, and rising debt issuance in particular. Third, greater
equity-market concentration increases exposure to a reassessment of technology
valuations. Together, these developments make a correction a clear risk.
To assess the potential for systemic stress, we apply the framework in Greenwood,

Hanson, Shleifer and Sørensen (GHSS). This associates heightened crisis risk with
the combination of a strong run-up in asset prices and excessive credit growth,
estimating a 45% probability of a crisis within 3 years if the two thresholds in Figure
11 are met. On a backward-looking basis as of 2025, an economy-wide assessment
finds that the equity-price increase meets the framework’s “red zone” test, but
business credit growth has been considerably more subdued, given the slowdown

### Page 11

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 11
in credit following the 2022-23 tightening cycle. That is, going into 2026, the pattern
was one of a displacement of credit from other interest-sensitive sectors towards
AI and technology rather than any broad-based rise in leverage.
Nevertheless, US data does show a clear acceleration in credit trends so far in 2026.
To make a more forward-looking assessment, we incorporate the rise in issuance

in 2026 and indicatively extend it into 2027 using the estimated rise in AI capex
plans, assuming credit growth elsewhere in the US economy remains stable
(footnote: We see two-sided risks to this – we may understate the broader credit
demand in tech adjacent areas; conversely, this may understate the drag of higher
rates on credit demand in non-AI related sectors). We also extend the estimated
2027 trend into 2028, including an assumption that equity prices continue their

trend over the past 12 months, to provide an illustrative scenario over the next two
years. Under these assumptions, we would see a visible rise in aggregate US
business sector leverage, leaving the sector just shy of economy-wide red-zone
levels by 2028. As such, approaching that territory by 2028 is conceivable if the AI
cycle continues to accelerate, both in terms of increased debt borrowing and a
renewed rise in valuations.
While the GHSS framework gives a useful quantification of the potential risks, its

indicators also warrant some qualification, which we judge to come in opposing
directions:
n On the one hand, there is a risk that aggregate credit measures may
understate vulnerabilities when borrowing is highly concentrated. Circular
financing, leases and off-balance-sheet structures complicate the picture,
and while we incorporate the recent rise in payables in our leverage metric
to capture some of this risk, it may well be underestimated. Moreover,

realised interest costs remain below current market borrowing rates after
the lengthy period of ultra-low rates, so leverage trends alone may miss the
pressure from rising debt-service costs.
n On the other hand, the price-based metric used by GHSS to assess excess
asset price growth (the 3-year real rise in equity prices) may overstate
valuation excesses given the strong rise in earnings. Indeed, P/E multiples
have already seen a sizeable correction since late 2025, which was most

pronounced for software stocks, and currently there is little evidence of
overly stretched tech valuations, especially when compared to the dot com
boom.
The risk of stress amplification is also likely to depend on the nature of any AI
correction. A downgrading of the AI capex cycle due to slower growth in AI
demand, financing constraints or supply bottlenecks may result in weaker
valuations, leading to negative wealth effects and slower capital expenditure.

However, while this would create an economic headwind, it need not produce a
systemic crisis. And while parallels to the dot com bubble may well be relevant,
currently there are factors that suggest downside risks would be more moderate.
For instance, operating cash flow still plays an important role in financing the AI
buildout, whilst there has been a limited run-up in borrowing and much less
stretched valuations relative to the late-1990s.
Our assessment therefore remains that an AI correction is a clearer risk than an AI-

led systemic crisis at this point. For these risks to become more material, it would
need leverage to keep building but AI returns to eventually disappoint, raising the
likelihood of credit events and amplifying the investment slowdown through the
real economy. We will focus on this interaction between debt-service pressures and

### Page 12

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 12 Deutsche Bank AG
the role of credit events more in the next section.

2. Risks from higher rates and private credit
In the previous section we noted that modest economy-wide debt growth doesn’t
necessarily imply modest credit risk when debt-service costs are rising. As existing
debt reprices or is refinanced, borrowers can face greater pressure even without
additional borrowing. This is a key feature of the debt cycle, whereby vulnerabilities
accumulate during the expansion, and rising rates (often amid a monetary

tightening due to an overheating economy) can then expose them. These risks are
relevant in the context of the current rate hiking cycle, especially as much of the
catch up in debt service costs is yet to play out (see chart below).
With banking-sector risks having moderated since the GFC, the rapid expansion of
private credit and its opaque structure have understandably drawn attention as a
possible source of the next crisis. Parallels with the subprime credit boom have

been periodically made. However, we think these should not be overstated. Even
though the dollar size of the US private credit market is similar to that of subprime
around 2006-07, the market must be assessed relative to the economy (which has
doubled in nominal terms). Moreover, the slower build-up, restrictions on
redemptions and lower risks of direct wealth effects all suggest much more
moderate amplification risks than in 2008.
There are already signs that the private-credit growth cycle is slowing (see chart)

and our credit strategists have noted warning signs including weaker new inflows
into BDCs, increasing payment-in-kind interest and prospective refinancing
pressures. That said, defaults remain limited thus far so these should not be seen
as evidence of an imminent systemic event.

### Page 14

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 14 Deutsche Bank AG
Whether more systemic risks could emerge will depend on issues of solvency,
opacity and capacity for intervention.
n First, while redemption gates can limit immediate cash outflows and
reduce the need to sell illiquid loans under pressure, they do not restore a
borrower’s ability to service debt or absorb underlying credit losses. This
distinction between liquidity protection and solvency is therefore central to

judging whether a slowing credit cycle remains orderly.
n Opacity remains a concern, both in financing structures and in the
distribution of exposures among end-holders, including insurers and other
non-banks. This can make losses harder to identify and potential
amplification harder to assess.
n Policymakers’ success in containing liquidity pressures may not translate
into an effective response to credit events - liquidity provision alone cannot

repair impaired balance sheets. Extending support to opaque non-bank
exposures raises difficult questions about eligibility, loss allocation and the
threshold for intervention.
Quite aside from private credit, the fact that we are back in a rate hiking cycle from
the major central banks also adds to the current risks. Indeed, higher rates bring
down inflation, in part because they tighten financial conditions. But a
consequence of this is that it often reveals vulnerabilities in the financial system,

and makes a crisis more likely. For instance, it was a contributing factor behind the
2023 regional banking crisis, as banks had growing unrealised losses on their
securities portfolio when rates began to rise again. So even if higher rates don’t
directly cause a crisis, they can often be a key contributor. Indeed, hiking cycles
have regularly coincided with some kind of shock, as the experience of recent
decades demonstrates.

3. Stretched fiscal positions and possible NBFI amplifiers
As discussed earlier, the migration of debt towards sovereign balance sheets has

helped cushion private-sector shocks, but this has come at the cost of higher fiscal
risks. Higher public debt and sovereign yields have limited the room for fiscal
stimulus, while persistent inflation has limited the room for monetary stimulus. This
makes another round of coordinated macroeconomic support harder to deliver.
The issue with high public debt is that it if fiscal sustainability is questioned, that can
raise sovereign borrowing costs, which can amplify existing private-sector

vulnerabilities via a tightening in financial conditions and slower growth.
The financial risk posed by sovereign stress will also depend on the financial
amplification channels. These were clear in the European sovereign crisis with the
bank-sovereign loop, but the changing structure of sovereign markets suggests
that the sovereign–NBFI feedback loop may now warrant increasing attention. This
was visible with the 2022 LDI crisis in the UK, while a more recent concern is the run

up in leveraged hedge fund positions in sovereign bond markets, with hedge funds’
sovereign bond exposures more than doubling since 2022 (see chart below). As
noted also by the IMF, this reflects greater issuance and a rising share of sovereign
debt held by hedge funds.
The lessons from Black Monday, LTCM and the LDI crisis offer reassurance that
intervention can contain market stress originating outside the banking system. But
they are not a guarantee that future liquidity shocks will remain economically

benign. The greater scale and interconnectedness of non-bank finance mean that
we can’t simply rely on historical examples today.
Looking forward, a combination of sovereign repricing, leveraged financial
amplification and a more constrained policy response risks proving harder to
resolve than past episodes.

Conclusion
Whilst a major financial crisis has been avoided since the GFC and the subsequent
sovereign crisis, the problem of financial instability has yet to be permanently

solved. On the one hand, reforms to the financial system and the swift actions of
policymakers have helped to prevent a repeat. But as we know from Hyman
Minsky’s warning, lengthy periods of stability can often lay the foundations for the
next crisis, even if these take place slowly. Indeed, risks often shift rather than
disappear.
Looking forward, there are several vectors of risk worth paying attention to. But
financial crises often start in areas where people are paying little attention, or risks

are assumed to be contained. Indeed, it is the areas where risk is underappreciated
which often prove the most vulnerable.
As such, predicting where the next crisis might arise is inherently difficult. But the
topic is a vital one, given the long-lasting damage that financial crises can cause
when they do occur. Indeed, with the fiscal space for sovereigns increasingly
constrained today, the margin for a future policy error is limited.

產業更新 – IP產業 
IP重要度提升，各家廠商積極串聯價值鏈  
What’s New –AI與先進製程提高 IP的策略價值 
大型 AI運算系統除了需要高效能運算核心，還需要 CPU負責控制與調度、HBM 提供高速資料
存取、SerDes 支援晶片及系統互連，以及安全模組進行身分驗證與金鑰保護，這意味著第三方
IP的商業機會並不只集中在單一晶片。但同時晶圓製程越先進，業者必須提前兩至三年投入研發，
卻因客戶專案延期而無法及時取得收入，短期獲利反而可能受到壓抑，研究部認為影響 IP 公司
競爭力的主要因素將是能否提早取得關鍵製程驗證、能否進入大型客戶的設計平台，以及技術投
入最終能否轉化為持續量產收入。 
Trends/Outlook –下游積極往上做併購取得關鍵 IP 
除了 IP供應商向下游擴張，晶片公司與晶圓代工廠也在向上游取得關鍵 IP。Qualcomm完成
收購 Alphawave Semi，強化高速傳輸、客製化晶片與資料中心產品能力。GlobalFoundries完
成收購 Synopsys的 ARC Processor IP 業務，並與 MIPS的 RISC-V、軟體及晶片開發能力結
合。皆說明 IP對下游業者的價值正在提高。反映晶圓代工與設計服務業者，也希望向客戶提供
更完整的晶片開發解決方案。因此未來產業競爭不只是IP公司彼此競爭，還包括晶片設計公司、
晶圓代工廠及 EDA業者在不同環節的跨界競爭。 
Investment Summary 
Synopsys、Cadence擁有龐大的全球客戶基礎，並能結合EDA工具、IP與驗證平台。ARM則
具有通用處理器架構及廣泛的軟體生態系。以台廠而言，M31(6643 TT) 主要產品涵蓋基礎元
件與高速介面 IP，並與多家晶圓代工廠合作開發不同製程的IP，面對AI ASIC與 Chiplet發展，
M31 有機會參與更多系統級晶片設計案。晶心科(6533 TT) 是台灣少數具有完整處理器 IP 產
品及相關工具生態系的上市公司，ARM直接跨足 CPU市場後，部分晶片設計業者可能更重視
供應商選擇與架構自主性，對 RISC-V形成間接利多。力旺(3529 TT) 其核心產品包括 OTP、
MTP、PUF等技術，可用於晶片內部資料儲存、修復、組態設定與安全用途。當ARM、Cadence
或其他大型供應商推出更完整的運算平台時，需要整合安全與記憶體功能，力旺已加入 ARM 
Total Design生態系，也參與Cadence的 Chiplet 合作平台，朝向更深入的系統級合作發展。

IP重要度提升，各家廠商積極串聯價值鏈 
AI與先進製程提高 IP的策略價值 
AI 加速器與客製化 ASIC的快速發展，使晶片設計從單一運算功能競爭，走向整體資料處理效率
競爭。例如一個大型AI運算系統除了需要高效能運算核心，還需要CPU負責控制與調度、HBM 
提供高速資料存取、SerDes支援晶片及系統互連，以及安全模組進行身分驗證與金鑰保護。 
這意味著第三方 IP 的商業機會並不只集中在單一晶片。但同時晶圓製程越先進，不代表每一家
IP公司一定越賺錢，業者必須提前兩至三年投入研發，卻因客戶專案延期而無法及時取得收入，
短期獲利反而可能受到壓抑，研究部認為 2027~2030 年影響 IP 公司競爭力的主要因素將是能
否提早取得關鍵製程驗證、能否進入大型客戶的設計平台，以及技術投入最終能否轉化為持續量
產收入。

表 1. IP類型區分

資料來源：國泰證期研究部整理

龍頭廠商 ARM開始進一步進化 
龍頭廠商 ARM推出 AGI CPU，首次提供自行設計的量產級晶片產品。該產品以Neoverse 架構
為基礎，針對代理式 AI資料中心的控制、調度與通用運算需求設計，早期與 Meta共同開發，
並獲得包括 OpenAI、Cloudflare等合作夥伴支持，在下半年已擴大供應，客戶 Meta訂單規模
具揭露達 10億美元。重要性不只是 ARM多了一項產品，過去 ARM的主要客戶是晶片設計廠
商，現在 ARM 可以直接向資料中心系統業者提供完整 CPU 產品，從供應鏈的上游技術環節，
延伸至更接近終端系統需求的位置。對半導體產業而言，最大的改變是 ARM不再只透過授權金
與權利金參與下游晶片市場，而是增加直接銷售硬體產品的機會。這意味著 ARM可掌握更多產
品定價、系統設計及客戶需求資訊，但也需要承擔製造協調、供應鏈、產品銷售及庫存等風險。

### Page 3

圖 1. ARM AGI CPU

資料來源：ARM、國泰證期研究部整理

其他 IP廠商也開始往系統級解決方案發展 
IP開始往下游擴張主因在於先進製程的設計、驗證及光罩成本增加，使客戶不願意承擔過高的開
發失敗風險。與其自行整合大量不同供應商的IP，部分客戶更願意採用已完成驗證的子系統或整
合平台，且客戶必須在有限時間內完成晶片設計及量產，若平台化 IP 能減少整合、除錯及驗證
時間，其價值就不只是單一 IP的效能，另外我們看到部分成熟的標準化 IP因替代供應商增加而
面臨價格壓力，供應商若能提供完整子系統、驗證服務及長期技術支援，就能提高產品差異化與
單一客戶的收入。

我們以 Synopsys與 Cadence來看，Synopsys近期宣布與 Amazon簽署超過 10億美元的多年
期合作協議，涵蓋客製化晶片的IP及 AI工程工具。這項合作的重要意義，在於IP供應商不一定
需要自己出售晶片，也能透過更深度參與客戶的晶片開發，擴大在價值鏈中取得的收益。傳統IP
公司通常在客戶設計階段提供技術模組，後續透過量產權利金取得收入，新的合作模式將 IP 供
應商與客戶的技術路線圖綁定得更緊密。Cadence 則推出 Chiplet 合作夥伴生態系，整合包括 
ARM、M31、力旺、Arteris 等供應商的 IP，並與 Samsung合作驗證平台，Cadence與 ARM 
有明顯不同，Cadence並不是要求所有 IP都由自己開發，而是透過設計工具、系統架構與預先
驗證流程，將不同廠商的技術整合成可讓客戶更快速使用的解決方案 ，從產業競爭角度來看，
Cadence掌握的是平台入口與整合能力，而其他專業 IP業者仍可透過合作取得市場機會。

### Page 4

投資 顧問 
產業報告

4 
研究部認為這是未來半導體 IP產業最值得關注的模式之一，產業領導者未必需要擁有每一項 IP，
但可能透過定義標準、整合流程與掌握客戶介面，提高其在生態系中的地位。但簡而言之，各家
IP廠商都在用自己的方式提高提供的價值鏈，突破傳統 IP收入模式框架。

下游亦積極往上做併購取得關鍵 IP 
除了 IP供應商向下游擴張，晶片公司與晶圓代工廠也在向上游取得關鍵 IP。Qualcomm完成收
購 Alphawave Semi，強化高速傳輸、客製化晶片與資料中心產品能力。GlobalFoundries完成
收購 Synopsys的 ARC Processor IP 業務，並與 MIPS的 RISC-V、軟體及晶片開發能力結合。
皆說明 IP 對下游業者的價值正在提高。Qualcomm 收購高速連接 IP，不只是增加可授權的產
品，而是為本身的 CPU、AI 加速器及資料中心平台建立更完整的技術能力。GlobalFoundries 
收購 ARC Processor IP，則反映晶圓代工與設計服務業者，也希望向客戶提供更完整的晶片開
發解決方案。因此未來產業競爭不只是 IP 公司彼此競爭，還包括晶片設計公司、晶圓代工廠及
EDA業者在不同環節的跨界競爭。

IP產業 2027年主要動能 
展望 2027年，研究部認為IP產業主要動能將隨著雲端服務業者自行開發更多晶片，CPU、加速
器、資料傳輸與記憶體架構的整合變得更加重要。客戶除了需要高效能IP，也需要降低晶片設計
風險與加快產品上市速度。這有利於具有完整技術組合，以及能提供預先驗證解決方案的供應商。
另外如同開頭前述，更先進製程的設計驗證成本增加，使具備成熟技術與晶圓代工廠合作關係的 
IP公司更具優勢，且 Chiplet 則使不同功能晶粒的互連、驗證及系統管理成為重要議題，將擴大 
Die-to-Die、PHY、記憶體介面 IP的需求。最後 AI伺服器需要在處理器、加速器、網路、儲存
與控制晶片之間建立一致的安全架構，這將為具備硬體信任根、PUF、OTP 等技術的業者提供龐
大機會。

相關 IP業者將顯著受惠 
Synopsys、Cadence擁有龐大的全球客戶基礎，並能結合 EDA工具、IP與驗證平台。ARM則
具有通用處理器架構及廣泛的軟體生態系。以台廠而言，M31(6643 TT) 主要產品涵蓋基礎元件
與高速介面 IP，並與多家晶圓代工廠合作開發不同製程的 IP，面對 AI ASIC 與 Chiplet發展，
M31有機會參與更多系統級晶片設計案。晶心科(6533 TT) 是台灣少數具有完整處理器 IP產品
及相關工具生態系的上市公司，ARM直接跨足 CPU市場後，部分晶片設計業者可能更重視供應
商選擇與架構自主性，對 RISC-V形成間接利多，CSP合作夥伴 Meta亦持續採用晶心科 IP產
品。力旺(3529 TT) 其核心產品包括 OTP、MTP、PUF 等技術，可用於晶片內部資料儲存、修
復、組態設定與安全用途。當 ARM、Cadence或其他大型供應商推出更完整的運算平台時，需
要整合安全與記憶體功能，力旺已加入 ARM Total Design生態系，也參與 Cadence的 Chiplet 
合作平台，策略正朝向更深入的系統級合作發展。

達發 6526 TT  ( 電子–半導體 ) 
光通發力，再達新高 
投資與評價 
1. 達發 3Q26 受惠光通與乙太網持續放亮，且商務耳機出貨暢旺，營收創
單季新高，獲利優於預期；預期 4Q26 光通與乙太網延續成長，淡季不
淡，營運為歷史同期新高。 
2. 單通道 100G 開始出貨，預期 27 年逐步放量，10G PON 切入 AI 資料
中心 OOB，新一代 PHY 同步貢獻，上修 26 年及 27 年營收預估，預估
26 年 EPS 21.76 元；27 年 EPS 30.18 元，營運創歷史新高。 
3. 目前達發股價為 707 元，約 23 倍 FY27E EPS ，位於近四年本益比參考
區間 20-50 倍偏下位置。考量光通高速成長，網通切入全新市場，維持
買進投資建議；惟受上下游供給緊缺，毛利率上升空間有限，將評價由
平均值 35 倍調至中間偏下 30 倍，目標價 900 元(30x FY27E EPS)。

重點更新 
光通與商務耳機出貨暢旺，達發 3Q26 營運創近四年新高 
達發 3Q26 光通憑藉產能與交期優勢提升市占，單通道 50G 出貨優於預期，
單通道 100G 開始小量出貨，乙太網受惠低軌衛星規格升級與交換器市占提
升，藍牙受惠旺季及高階專案出貨，營收季增 10.4%至 69.41 億元，創單季
新高。毛利率 49.3%優於原先預估之 49.1%，費用方面控管得當，營業費用
22.87 億元(+0.6% QoQ)低於原先預估之 23.35 億元，營業利益 11.34 億元
(+20.3% QoQ)，稅後淨利季增 17.0%至 10.29 億元，EPS 6.15 元，營運創
近四年單季新高。

預期光通與乙太網延續成長，達發 4Q26 營運為歷史同期新高 
達發 4Q26 提前拉貨比重有限 ， 預期藍牙受惠商務耳機量產 ， 跌幅小於往年；
光通單通道 100G 逐漸放量，乙太網 2.5G PHY 與交換器延續成長，因此上
修營收預估 5.3%至 67.80 億元(-2.3% QoQ)，淡季不淡。 預估毛利率47.8%，
營業費用 21.60 億元(-5.6% QoQ)，營業利益 10.83 億元(-4.5% QoQ)，稅
後淨利 9.88 億元(-4.1% QoQ)，EPS 5.90 元，營運為歷史同期新高。

### Page 2

報告內容 
光通與商務耳機出貨暢旺，達發 3Q26 獲利優於預期 
達發 3Q26 光通需求強勁，在市場供給偏緊下，達發產能與交期相對穩定，單通道 50G 持續提
升市占，出貨優於預期，單通道 100G 亦開始小量出貨，50G TIA 並以獨立晶片形式出貨。乙太
網受惠低軌衛星客戶由 1G 升級至 ASP 較高的 2.5G PHY，輕管理型交換器需求增溫。寬頻以北
美成長最快，10G PON 比重逐步升高。藍牙則為消費旺季，電競及商務耳機等高階專案出貨增
加，3Q26 營收季增 10.4%至 69.41 億元(+23.3% YoY)，創單季新高。 
毛利率方面，DRAM 及 NOR Flash 今年漲幅達數倍，封測成本亦上升，產品漲價於 3Q26 貢獻
有限，惟產品組合優化部分抵減成本壓力，毛利率季減1.9 個百分點至 49.3%，略優於原先預估
之 49.1%。營業費用 22.87 億元(+0.6% QoQ)，低於原先預估之 23.35 億元，營業利益11.34 億
元(+20.3% QoQ)，稅後淨利季增 17.0%至 10.29 億元，EPS 6.15 元，營運創近四年單季新高。

### Page 3

光通成長淡季不淡，預期達發 4Q26 營運為歷史同期新高 
達發 4Q26 有別於傳統淡季，預期光通與乙太網延續強勁成長，其中光通單通道 100G 出貨增
加，為最大成長動能，乙太網則受惠2.5G PHY 持續導入 Wi-Fi 新客戶及新一代 10G PHY 量產。
寬頻隨北美設備商產品陸續落地 ， 並切入AI 資料中心 OOB 新市場 ， 藍牙則受惠商務耳機專案量
產，高階應用占比提升，預估淡季影響優於往年，加上下半年提前拉貨占藍牙音訊及寬頻營收皆
不到一成 ， 後續客戶庫存調整壓力有限 ， 上修4Q26 營收預估 5.3%至 67.80 億元(-2.3% QoQ)，
淡季不淡。 
毛利率方面，產品價格調整預期自 4Q26 開始反映，光通占比持續提升，惟調價依客戶及產品線
逐案協商，預期無法完全反映成本漲幅，且 NOR Flash 及封測成本持續走揚，下修毛利率預估
1.3 個百分點至 47.8%(-1.5ppts QoQ)。下調營業費用 2.1%至 21.60 億元(-5.6% QoQ)，上調營
業利益預估 13.2%至 10.83 億元(-4.5% QoQ, +127.1% YoY)； 稅後淨利9.88 億元(-4.1% QoQ，
+78.0% YoY)，EPS 5.90 元，營運為歷史同期新高。

光通營收大幅成長，預期達發 26 年營運創歷史新高 
達發 26 年光通受惠全球前十大模組廠滲透率提升 ， 加上AI 資料中心光模組進入三至五年替換週
期，預期營收成長至 25 年的四倍以上，占全年營收 mid-single digit ，產品以 12 奈米單通道
50G 為主，單通道100G 則於下半年開始小量出貨。寬頻繼25 年歐洲市場放量後，26 年由北美
接棒，1~3Q26 北美營收翻倍成長，10G PON 增速領先其他寬頻產品，乙太網則受惠低軌衛星
地面設備價量齊揚，非管理型交換器海外通路市占穩健擴張，預期為成長僅次於光通的產品線，
藍牙商務耳機 26 年營收預期翻倍成長，占藍牙音訊比重由個位數提升至接近兩成，帶動ASP 提
升，上修 26 年營收預估 1.4%至 254.66 億元(+21.7% YoY)。 
毛利率方面，1H26 受惠產品組合維持於 51.9%，惟下半年上下游成本皆大幅上漲，下修毛利率
預估 0.3 個百分點至 50.1%(-1.8ppts YoY)。下調營業費用預估 1.0%至 87.82 億元(+10.4% YoY)，
費用率因營收規模放大，由 25 年的 38.0%降至 34.5%，營業利益 39.76 億元(+36.8% YoY)；預
估稅後淨利年增 25.7%至 36.45 億元，EPS 21.76 元，營運創歷史新高。

### Page 4

100G 接棒放量，預期達發 27 年營運再創歷史新高 
達發 27 年光通動能由單通道 100G 接棒，驗證進度符合預期， 1H27 逐步放量，預估於 2Q27
占光通營收一半，100G DSP ASP 為 50G 的兩倍以上，100G TIA 搭載率預期高於 50G。預期 27
年光通營收成長至 26 年的三倍，占營收比重提升至 15%。中長期則有單通道 200G 目標於 27
年底 T ape Out，並投資元澄半導體以加速模組驗證與導入。寬頻方面，25G 及 50G PON 預期
於 27 年量產，OOB 目前以較低 PON 數配置為主，配置擴大後滲透率可望提升。乙太網新一代
10G PHY 搭配 10G PON、Wi-Fi 及 5G FWA 平台，預期 2.5G 交換器出貨增加。藍牙 6 奈米新
平台預期 26 年底 T ape Out，並往 B2B 應用布局，上修 27 年營收預估 3.6%至 306.74 億元
(+20.5% YoY)。 
毛利率方面，預期產品價格調整效益 27 年逐步顯現，光通占比亦持續提升，惟使用封裝及記憶
體較多之藍牙與寬頻等產品預估合計仍占營收七至八成，且達發身為後進者，光通定價以擴大市
占為優先，下修毛利率預估 0.9 個百分點至 50.1%，與 26 年持平。研發費用隨 100G、200G 
SerDes 及 25G PON 等新產品持續投入，惟 200G SerDes IP 授權金認列為無形資產並分期攤
銷，對費用影響有限，預估營業費用97.48 億元(+11.0% YoY)，營業費用率下降至 31.8%，營業
利益 56.10 億元(+41.1% YoY)；預估稅後淨利年增 38.7%至 50.56 億元，EPS 30.18 元，營運再
創歷史新高。

100G 放量在即，達發成長動能升溫 
達發過去以消費性藍牙與家用寬頻為主，目前有線網通基礎建設營收占比已達約五成，預期 27
年持續提升，AI 資料中心相關營收比重加速擴大。高速光通、OOB 及乙太網 PHY 同步切入 AI
基礎建設，客戶由模組廠延伸至大型雲端業者，產品週期波動逐步降低，預估26 年及 27 年 EPS
分別為 21.76 元及 30.18 元，成長動能明確。 
目前達發股價為 707 元(23X FY27E EPS)，位於近四年本益比參考區間 20-50 倍下緣位置，考量
光通成長動能預期延續，OOB 架構打開 AI 資料中心新應用，維持買進投資評等。惟 27 年毛利
率預估持平於 50.1%，毛利率仍受上下游供給緊缺影響，因此將評價基礎由平均值之 35 倍調整
至中間偏下之 30 倍，目標價由 995 元(35x 修正前 FY27E EPS)調整至 900 元(30x 修正後 FY27E 
EPS)。

### Page 5

公司簡介 
達發科技股份有限公司(Airoha Technology Corp., 6526 TT) 成立於 2001 年，總部位於新竹縣
竹北市，為聯發科集團旗下 IC 設計公司。2021 年整併誠致科技、雷凌科技、絡達科技及聯發科
IoT 部門並更名為達發科技，2022 年 6 月登錄興櫃，2023 年 10 月於證交所上市。公司業務分
為有線網通基礎建設與無線邊緣 AI 兩大事業群：有線網通涵蓋固網寬頻(G-PON、10G PON)、
乙太網(PHY 與交換器晶片)及光通訊(PAM4 DSP 與 TIA)，應用由家用寬頻、低軌衛星地面設備
延伸至 AI 資料中心光通訊與帶外管理，並於 2024 年 7 月完成參與九暘私募，九暘為合併子公
司，布局 PoE 晶片；無線邊緣 AI 以藍牙音訊為主，聚焦商務、電競及聲學等中高階耳機市場，
並延伸至藍牙傳輸(電競鍵鼠、電子貨架標籤)及衛星定位(GNSS)晶片。目前有線網通基礎建設與
無線邊緣 AI 兩大事業群營收占比各約 50%。

圖三 : 達發 3Q26 營收占比 
資料來源:達發，國泰證期研究部整理

### Page 6

ESG 政策 
達發以社會、經濟、環境及資訊揭露四大構面推動永續經營，已在員工照護、職業安全、董
事會獨立性、資訊安全及溫室氣體管理等面向建立相關制度，評估期間未發生重大人權、消
費者權益、職業災害、誠信經營及環境法規違規情事。

社會面：達發依循國際人權宣言制定人權政策並揭露執行結果，並將人權要求納入供應商評
鑑與稽核。員工管理方面，提供優於法規的休假、保險及身心健康措施，並以員工認股權與
持股信託強化人才留任；職業安全衛生管理系統已取得外部認證，並揭露工傷統計及減災目
標。另建立績效評估及能力發展計畫，設定員工多元化目標、揭露女性主管占比，並足額進
用身心障礙員工。未來可完善人權盡職調查流程，並揭露員工滿意度、訓練成效、性別薪酬
比及客戶滿意度。

經濟面：達發每年召開 2 次以上法人說明會，董事長親自出席股東常會，並揭露議案表決結
果。獨立董事席次達半數以上，設置永續發展相關委員會並由董事長擔任最高負責人，且定
期執行董事會外部績效評估。另制定反貪腐、反競爭、內線交易防範及檢舉制度，風險管理
政策經董事會通過，並設置資安專責單位、定期測試資安機制。後續可強化接班規劃、設置
提名委員會、提高任一性別董事席次至三分之一以上，並將永續績效納入高階主管與董事薪
酬。

環境面：氣候管理由董事會及管理階層監督，已揭露範疇一、二及範疇三排放，並設定量化
中長期減碳、節能及再生能源目標。達發亦制定水資源及廢棄物管理政策，揭露用水量及有
害廢棄物產出量，並將環境表現納入供應商遴選及稽核。惟尚未取得 ISO 14001 及碳盤查認
證，未來可補充 1.5°C 以下及 3°C 以上氣候情境分析、再生能源使用量及用水、廢棄物量化
減量目標。

揭露面：達發依循 GRI 準則編製永續報告書，揭露重大性分析及 SDGs 對應作為，並提供英
文版報告書及英文網站。後續可導入 SASB 對應揭露及第三方確信，建立利害關係人回饋機
制，並採用雙重重大性原則強化 ESG 資訊完整性。

歐洲投資級企業獲利強健，利
息成本短期難改善
熱門議題 / 投資關鍵
《AI發債引發債務可持續性擔憂》
近期公債殖利率攀高引發市場對 AI融資需求以及
企業償債能力的擔憂 ，AI產業鏈信用利差與 CDS
擴大，不過多數大型科技企業多為投資級 ，美國
投資級AI產業鏈信用利差水準仍未突破今年高點；
此外統計今年以來大量發債的美國投資級債發行
人(淨發行量100億美元以上)共有17家，其中17%
的發行人信評獲調升 ，33%被下調 ，50%不變，
反映債務增加尚未造成普遍的債信壓力。

市場投資展望
資產 4Q26看法 投資展望
全球投資級債指數 正向 / 5.35 長期投資人可考慮鎖利取得豐沛孳息
美國投資級債指數 正向 / 5.80 長期投資人可考慮鎖利取得豐沛孳息
歐洲投資級債指數 正向 / 4.20 長期投資人可考慮鎖利取得豐沛孳息
全球非投資級債指數 中性 / 7.40 聚焦貨幣政策與企業債信變化，預期指數表現以孳息為主
美國非投資級債指數 正向/ 7.65 信評BB級以上之中大型企業債較為穩健
歐洲非投資級債指數 中性 / 6.10 違約率中期有望緩解，以現金流穩健發行人為主

資結果自行負責。未經本公司許可，本資料及訊息不得逕行抄錄、翻印或另作派發。本行以上市場資訊與分析，不涉及特定投資標的之建議。
重點摘要 穆迪統計五大超大規模資料中心 (微軟、Meta、谷
歌、亞馬遜 、甲骨文 )因獲利強勁 ，平均槓桿率為
2.1倍，而10年平均2.7倍，是自2013年以來的最低
水平，償債能力尚無疑慮；研判近期部分科技業利
差擴大主要反映市場對 AI經濟效益的重新評估 ，以
及對評級較低 、資本密集度較高的發行人要求更高
的風險溢酬 ，並非潛在的信用風險事件或整體信用
基本面惡化 ，不過仍須留意目前總體經濟環境對於
信用評級較低的發行人而言更容易受到長期高利率
和成本壓力的影響 ，高低評等發行人的市場表現分
歧或將加大。
另一方面，回顧2026年第三季美投資級債評等變動
不多，2026年迄今的評級上調 /下調比率為2.3倍；
雖然這一比率低於 2022-2024年期間強勁的升評趨

勢，但高於2025年，且已是第六年大於一倍 ，反映
投資級債整體信用環境維持良好。

重點摘要
• 法國主要銀行包括法國巴黎
銀行以及興業銀行 3Q26獲利
狀況良好 、資產品質未顯著
惡化
《歐洲信用債市受法國財政疑慮波及》
歐洲信用市場雖受 AI相關債券波動影響較小 ，但因
能源價格高企 ，加上近期法國主權和財政風險加劇 ，
歐洲信用債市 十月份表現也有不小波動 ，尤其是非
投資級債信用利差一度擴大逾 60個基點。歐洲銀行
債是各產業信用債主權風險曝險較高的類別 ，因歐
洲銀行多數不及美國銀行營運遍及全球且業務多元 ，
此外也持有不少主權債資產 ，十月份市場表現也反
映此一狀況 ，主權債的波動更加重了歐洲銀行債的
跌幅，法國銀行債利差擴大幅度最為明顯 ，所幸法
國主要銀行包括法國巴黎銀行以及興業銀行3Q26獲

利狀況良好 ，即使經營環境惡化 ，從不良貸款率與
信用風險成本等資產品質指標來看法國巴黎銀行持
平前季、法國興業銀行僅微幅上升。
目前市場關注的焦點仍是法國財政整頓的路徑 ，法
國目前的債務負擔約佔 GDP的118%至119%，高盛
預計，如果沒有更有效的財政調整 ，到2030年初負
債比率將進一步上升至接近 125%，如要穩定債務
佔GDP的比重，法國需要實現約佔 GDP 1%的財政
盈餘，但目前的財政赤字約佔 2.5% GDP。法國政
府當局可能在年底前通過一項撙節的預算案 ，若沒
通過法國主權債與信用債可能進一步惡化 ，但即使
通過法國主權債利率要大幅回落的機會仍不大 ，可
能要到2027年法國大選後主權債才有機會趨於穩定 ，
屆時法國銀行債或有布局機會。
歐洲可供吸收損失銀行債信用利差(基點)
資料來源：高盛，2026/10，中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/10
法國銀行債受主權債重新定價帶來的衝擊影響
法國 英國 西班牙 德國

### Page 4

不做任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行並不負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資料而做出或改變投資決策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資之風險，並就投
資結果自行負責。未經本公司許可，本資料及訊息不得逕行抄錄、翻印或另作派發。本行以上市場資訊與分析，不涉及特定投資標的之建議。
重點摘要
• 高利率持續下信用基本面都
已 出 現分 化，如CCC等級的
償債能力指標顯著疲弱
• 衡量整體債信風險仍是歐洲
較美國為高
《非投資級債美國仍優於歐洲》
儘管目前不論是美國還是歐洲的非投資級債在高利
率持續下信用基本面都已出現分化 ，如CCC等級的
償債能力 指標顯著疲弱 ，但衡量整體債信風險仍是
歐洲較美國為高；原因包括歐洲的債務到期牆較美
國明顯，且美國非投資級債2028年前到期的債券分
佈在相對廣泛的行業 ，而當前的賤售債券主要集中
在媒體、原物料與電信 、非必需消費等特定領域 ，
上述較少重疊降低落入賤售債券利差區間的發行人
同時面臨債務到期後償債或再融資令違約風險增加
的可能。另一方面 ，歐洲非投資級債的到期牆更為

陡峭，且市場利率與2028年前到期債券的票面利率
差距大於美非投資級債 ，反映再融資壓力更鉅 ，尤
其是歐洲CCC等級。
分析歐洲非投資級債各產業債務到期狀況可發現
2028年前到期債務產業相對集中 ，且易受景氣影響
的非必需消費在到期債務與賤售債券都佔有一定比
例，另外槓桿率本就較高的電信業佔 2028年前到期
債務9%，融資成本大幅上漲恐令這些發行人債信體
質進一步惡化。
資料來源：高盛，2026/10，中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/10
資料來源：高盛，2026/10，中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/10

中国の成長分野における赤字企業の増加
中国では、 赤字企業が増加している。 とりわけ、 成長分野における増加が顕著である。 この要因の一つは、
し烈な企業間競争による選別である。また、赤字でも潜在力が評価される次世の成長企業も多い。
■成長分野における赤字企業の増加
近年、中国では成長分野における赤字企業の
増加が顕著である。上場企業数に占める赤字企
業の比率（赤字企業比率）は2019年の 9.6％か
ら 2025年に 26.9％へ急上昇している （右上図）。
赤字企業の業種分布をみると、2019～22 年は、
新型コロナウイルス対策として実施された厳格
な外出規制や不動産不況を受けて、飲食・宿泊
業上場企業（以下、上場企業を省略）や不動産
業など非製造業を中心に増加した。 一方で、2022
～25 年には、情報通信機器・電子部品・デバイ
ス、はん用・生産用・業務用機械、電気機械、

化学工業で赤字企業が急増している。これら 4
分野だけで全体の上昇分の約6割を説明できる。
これらの分野は中国製造業の代表的な成長分
野である。実際、この 4 分野の付加価値額の増
加率は、いずれも中国製造業の平均値を上回る。
中国政府が重点的に育成してきたハイテク製造
業も、 これらの分野に多数含まれる。 たとえば、
5G基地局や半導体は、 情報通信機器・電子部品・
デバイスに分類される。ロボットは、はん用・
生産用・業務用機械に含まれる。太陽電池や蓄
電池は電気機械、バイオ医薬品や有機化学素材
は化学工業に属している。
情報通信機器・電子部品・デバイス製造業の
近年の動きをみると、2019年頃から世界的に通信規格が4G（LTE）から 5G サービスへの移行
がみられ、2022年 11 月に ChatGPTが公開された。これらが、同分野の成長の追い風となって
いる。 足元では、 一般の消費者や企業がAIサービスを日常的に利用できることが示されたことで、
AI向け端末や半導体、 データセンターの需要が急増している。 こうしたなか、 多くの企業が開発・

製造・販売の強化に注力しており、 同分野の付加価値額は2022年の7,800億元から2025年に9,100
億元へと増加し続けている。その一方で、赤字企業数は同期間に113社から 198社へ増加し、赤
字企業比率は17.2％から 30.1％へと急上昇している（右下図）。
■企業間競争を通じた選別
成長分野でありながら赤字企業が増加している理由の一つは、し烈な企業間競争である。市場
シェアを巡って、設備投資競争や価格競争が過熱している。情報通信機器・電子部品・デバイス

製造業についてみると、2022年から 2025年の
期間における固定資産残高の増加率（＋35.4％）
は、付加価値額の増加率（＋16.9％）を大きく
上回る。これは、供給能力が需要を上回るベー
スで拡大していることを示している。また、消
費者物価指数（CPI）は同期間に＋0.4％とほぼ
横ばい、そのうちサービス価格は賃金上昇など
を反映して同＋2.2％上昇している一方で、財
（モノ）の価格は製造業における供給超過によ
り同▲0.7％下落している。情報通信機器価格の
下落幅は▲3.5％と財全体と比べても大きい。
企業間競争を通じた選別が進んだ結果、利益
は競争力の高い少数企業に集中している。情報通信機器・電子部品・デバイス製造業の利益総額
に占めるトップ1％企業の利益の割合が2022年の 24.0％から 2025年に 43.3％へと、 大きく上昇
している（右上図）。有力企業は「国内チャンピオン企業」であると同時に「グローバル・チャ

ンピオン企業」としてのポジションを形成しつつある。
■赤字でも潜在力が評価される次代の成長企業
ただし、赤字企業を敗者としてひとくくりにすることはできない。近年、世界的には赤字であ
っても成長性が高い企業に対する投資家の関心が高まっており、中国においても同様な赤字企業
が少なからず存在している。
情報通信機器・電子部品・デバイス製造業に
ついてみると、赤字企業の2 社のうち 1 社は投
資家が高い関心を示している企業である。上海
総合株価指数は、2023年初の 3,089ポイントか
ら2025年末に3,969ポイントへと＋28.5％上昇
した。2025年の赤字企業198社のうち 2023年
時点で上場していなかった 20 社を除くと 178
社となる。この178社のうち 96社（53. 9％）
の当該期間における株価上昇率は、上海総合株
価指数の上昇率を上回る（右下図）。
この相対株価パフォーマンスの良い 96 社の
特徴の一つは、研究開発や無形資産への投資に
積極的である点である。96社の 2022年の R&D

集約度（研究開発投資／売上高）の平均値は
9.6％、無形資産残高の対付加価値額比率も60.5％に達している。無形資産には、コンピュータの
ソフトウェア、特許、著作権、顧客や仕入れ先との関係などが含まれる。これに対し、相対株価
パフォーマンスの悪い赤字企業82 社の R&D 集約度は 6.5％、無形資産残高の対付加価値額比率
は 38.9％にとどまる。
このように、中国の成長分野における赤字企業の増加は顕著であり、その背景には企業間競争
による選別だけでなく、潜在力が評価される次代の成長企業が生まれていることも大きい。

気候変動が高めるアジアの成長停滞リスク
気候変動は、労働生産性や資本蓄積の停滞を通じて ASEAN・インドの成長停滞リスクを高め得る。その
軽減・回避に向け、官民による「適応投資」を促す事業環境と制度の整備が急務である。
■アジアで深刻化する大雨・洪水被害
アジアでは近年、 人的被害を伴う大規模な大雨
や洪水が頻発している（右上表）。記憶に新しい
のは、本年 8月にネパールと中国の国境付近で発
生した土石流であり、本稿執筆時点では死者・行
方不明者が数千人に上る大災害となっている。ま
た、昨年11月にも東南アジア・南アジアで記録
的な豪雨が発生し、 インドネシアとスリランカを
中心に多数の犠牲者が出た。
こうした洪水・大雨のリスクを高める要因の一
つが、気候変動である。気温が上昇すると大気に
含まれる水蒸気量が増えるため、 地球温暖化は豪
雨の激甚化をもたらし得る。 こうしたリスクはア

ジア全体に共通するものだが、 その影響の受け方
は地域によって異なる。なかでもASEAN・イン
ドは、モンスーン（季節風）の影響を強く受け、
雨季に降水が集中する地域が多い。 こうした地域
では、 温暖化によりモンスーンがもたらす大雨をさらに強め、 洪水や土砂災害のリスクを高める。
■気候変動が遠ざける「中所得国の罠」からの脱却
気候変動は、自然災害による人的被害をもたらすのみならず、様々な経路を通じてASEAN・
インド各国の長期的な経済成長力を損なう可能性がある。ADB［2024］は、気候変動がアジア各
国の経済を長期的にどの程度下押しするかについて、定量的な分析を行った。この分析は、IPCC
の「温室効果ガス高排出シナリオ（SSP5-8.5）」に基づき、産業革命以前と比較して2100 年ま
でに地球の平均気温が4.7℃上昇すると仮定したうえで、気候変動がなかった場合の実質GDPと
の差分を推計したものである。推計結果は、気候変動が招く海水面上昇、洪水、熱波などの事象
が、労働生産性の低下や資本蓄積の停滞、消費の

下押しを通じて経済成長に及ぼす長期的な影響
を示している。
結果を見ると、各国・地域とも気候変動がなか
った場合と比べた実質GDPの下押し幅は、2035
年、2050年、2070年と時間の経過とともに拡大
していくことがわかる （右下図）。また、ASEAN・
インドの下押し幅は、2050年時点で▲15.0～▲
8.8％、2070年時点で▲30.2～▲18.1％と、中国
（2050 年：▲4.8％、2070 年：▲10.3％）や中
主任研究員 細井 友洋
(hosoi.tomohiro@jri.co.jp）

JAPAN RESEARCH INSTITUTE
4
央アジア諸国（2050年：▲1.7％、2070 年：▲
3.8％）に比べて大きい。ASEAN・インドはアジ
アのなかでも気候変動による経済停滞リスクが
相対的に大きい地域であると言えよう。
このことは、ASEAN・インドが直面する「中
所得国の罠」 からの脱却を一段と難しくするおそ
れがある。これらの地域では、所得水準は上昇し
ているものの、 高所得国の基準にはなお距離があ
る（右上図）。1990年代またはそれ以前に中所
得国となったインドネシア、マレーシア、フィリ
ピン、タイは、長らく高所得国入りを果たせてい
ない。また、2000 年代以降に中所得国となった
ベトナムとインドも、 中国がかつて経験したよう
な高成長軌道には乗れていない。気候変動は、気
温上昇による労働生産性の停滞や災害の頻発化
による投資抑制などを通じて、ASEAN・インド
の所得向上をさらに遅らせる要因となり得る。

■「適応投資」の加速に向けた環境整備が重要
こうした気候変動による成長下押しを緩和す
るには、官民による「適応投資」の拡大が不可欠
である。具体的には、気温上昇に伴う労働生産性
の低下を和らげる労働環境の改善や省力化・省人化、海水面上昇・洪水による生産設備への被害
を防ぐインフラ整備・設備強靭化などが挙げられる。もっとも、ASEAN・インドは、こうした適
応投資を推進する官民の基盤が十分に整っているとは言いがたい。
米ノートルダム大学は「グローバル適応イニシアティブ（ND-GAIN）」のなかで、投資を活用
して気候変動への適応につなげる国の能力を「適応準備度」として指標化しており、経済、ガバ
ナンス、社会の 3 項目から評価を行っている。それぞれ、①民間企業が適応投資を行いやすい事
業環境が準備されているか、②官民の投資を支える制度や政治の実効性が確保されているか、③
投資を促進するソフト・ハード面での社会インフラが整っているか、を評価するものである。
2024年の適応準備度を見ると、ASEAN・インドの世界順位は総じて中位～下位圏にとどまり、

先進国との差が大きい（右下表）。とりわけ、フィリピン、インド、タイは100位圏外に位置し、
適応投資の推進力が弱いことがうかがえる。内訳を見ると、とくに経済面とガバナンス面に問題
がみられる。インドとタイの経済準備度はそれぞれ113 位、124 位と際立って低く、ガバナンス
準備度もマレーシアを除く5 カ国で 100 位圏外にとどまる。これらを踏まえると、インドとタイ
ではとくに、民間企業による適応投資の加速に向けて、必要資金を内外から調達しやすくする金
融環境整備、新事業立ち上げを促進する規制の緩和、人材を円滑に確保できる労働市場改革など
を急ぐ必要がある。加えて、地域全体として、政策の安定性向上や法制度の実効性確保、汚職の
抑制など、官民投資を支える制度・政治の信頼性を高めることも重要である。
（参考文献）

ASIA MONTHLY 2026 年 10 月（No.307）
JAPAN RESEARCH INSTITUTE
5
韓 国  景気回復の勢い、年後半は鈍化
■AI需要を追い風に景気は回復
韓国景気は回復している。実質GDPは 1～3月
期に大きく増加した後、4～6月期も前期比＋0.6％
とプラス成長を維持した（右上図）。
外需が景気回復をけん引している。AI利用の拡
大、とりわけ推論需要の増加に伴い、韓国企業が
強みを持つメモリ半導体への需要が急増している。
これを背景に、輸出額は過去最高水準で推移して
いる。
個人消費は良好な所得環境や政府の支援策に支
えられ、緩やかに増加している。大手企業では、
高水準の賃上げやボーナスの増加を背景に、上半
期の平均給与が前年同期比＋ 7.1％と大きく増加
した。一方、雇用の多くを占める中小企業では、
賃金がほとんど増加しておらず、所得格差が拡大
している。こうしたなか、政府は中東情勢悪化に
よる物価高の影響を和らげるため、4月から所得下
位 7 割を対象に最大約6 万円相当の支援金を給付

した。これにより中低所得層の購買力が下支えさ
れ、中東危機による消費への悪影響が緩和された。
設備投資も増加している。AI需要の拡大を背景に、半導体関連産業で設備投資が活発化してい
ることに加え、研究開発やソフトウェア投資も増加している。
■個人消費の減速が景気の重石に
年後半も韓国経済は回復基調を維持し、 今年の実質GDP成長率は＋3.2％に達すると予想する。
AI投資の収益性を巡る懸念は根強いものの、 海外のデータセンター投資は引き続き拡大しており、
半導体を中心としたAI関連財の輸出は高水準を維持する見込みである。
もっとも、個人消費は徐々に減速する見通しである。政府による支援金の押し上げ効果がはく
落することに加え、エネルギー価格の高騰が幅広い財・サービス価格に転嫁され、家計の購買力
が圧迫されると予想する。
足元の消費者物価は前年比＋3％前後と、金融政策運営上の物価目標である＋2％を上回ってい
る。エネルギー価格の高止まりに加え、パソコンやスマートフォンなど半導体搭載製品の価格が

上昇していることが背景にある。インフレ抑制に向けて、韓国銀行は7 月から 2 カ月連続で政策
金利を 0.25％ポイントずつ引き上げ、3.0％とした。
反面、急速な利上げで景気が下押しされるリスクもある。韓国では諸外国と比べて家計債務残
高が大きく、変動金利型ローンの割合も高い（右下図）。金利上昇による利払い負担の増加は、
家計の可処分所得を減少させ、個人消費を下押しする恐れがある。さらに、資金調達の難化や建
設コストの上昇を通じて、建設不況が長期化する可能性もある。実際、建設会社の廃業申請数は
前年から 1割以上増加している。
研究員 今川 冬馬（imaga wa.thoma@jri.co.jp）

6
インドネシア 景気は堅調も、気候要因が先行きの重石に
■4～6月期は 5％台の成長を維持
インドネシアの2026年 4～6月期の実質GDP
成長率は前年同期比＋5.3％と、 前期（同＋5.6％）
からは減速したものの、なお堅調な伸びを維持
している（右上図）。需要項目別にみると、個
人消費が小幅に減速した。原油高に伴うインフ
レの高進が、家計の実質所得を下押ししたこと
が背景にある。一方、プラボウォ政権の看板政
策である 「無料給食プログラム」 などを背景に、
政府消費は同＋16.0％と、前期（同＋21.8％）に
引き続き高い伸びとなった。投資の拡大を背景
に、総固定資本形成は同＋6.9％と、前期の同＋
6.0％から加速した。
外需の面では、輸出が同＋4.1％と、前期（同
＋0.9％）から大幅に加速した。パーム油やニッ
ケル関連製品などの財輸出が増加したことに加
え、インバウンドの回復に伴いサービス輸出も

高い伸びを維持した。他方、設備投資の拡大に
伴い、生産用原材料や中間財の輸入も急増した
ため、 純輸出の成長率寄与度はマイナスとなっ
た。
■気候要因が先行きの景気を下押し
先行き、中東危機に伴うエネルギー価格の高止まりが幅広い財・サービス価格に転嫁されるこ
とで、物価上昇圧力が強まると予想する。加えて、気候要因も内需の重石になるとみられる。
インドネシア気象庁（BMKG）によると、8 月の Niño3.4（エルニーニョ現象やラニーニャ現
象の発生を監視・判断するための指標）は＋2.8℃に達し、エルニーニョ現象は極めて強い状態に
ある（右下図）。また、インド洋東西の海面水温差を示すインド洋ダイポールモード指数（IOD
指数）も＋0.38℃と、正のIODが発生する基準値の目安とされる＋0.4℃付近まで上昇した。世界
気象機関（WMO）は、今後、正のIODが形成されると予測している。エルニーニョに正のIOD
が重なれば、インドネシア周辺の対流活動が弱まり、少雨傾向が一段と強まるとみられる。
こうした気候条件が続けば、森林火災が発生しやすくなるほか、干ばつによる農産物生産の下

振れを通じて、食料品を中心に物価上昇圧力が高まる。8 月の消費者物価はすでに前年同月比＋
3.2％と、物価目標レンジの上限に近く、一段と上昇すれば、家計の実質購買力が低下し、個人消
費の伸びが鈍化すると見込まれる。さらに、干ばつによって国内供給が不足すれば、食料品輸入
の増加が経常赤字の拡大を招き、ルピア安圧力を強める。インドネシア銀行（中央銀行）は通貨
防衛を目的に金融引き締め姿勢を続けるとみられ、物価高・ルピア安・金利高が同時に進行する
場合、家計の耐久財消費や住宅購入、企業の設備投資を一段と下押しすると見込まれる。
研究員 呉 子婧（ wu.zijing@jri.co.jp）

JAPAN RESEARCH INSTITUTE
7
マレーシア 外需主導で足元の景気は持ち直し
■輸出が成長をけん引
マレーシア経済は、外需の拡大を主因に成長
ペースが高まっている。2026年 4～6 月期の実
質 GDP成長率は前年同期比＋6.0％と、 前期 （同
＋5.4％）から加速した（右上図）。
需要項目別にみると、外需が景気をけん引し
ている。世界的なAIブームを背景に、電子機器
や関連部品の輸出が好調に推移したことに加え、
液化天然ガス（LNG）の出荷も回復しつつある
ことから、財輸出は同＋16.8 ％と前期（同＋
2.5％）から大幅に拡大した。サービス輸出も、
外国人旅行者の増加や旅行支出の拡大を受けて、
同＋18.0％と高成長を維持した。
こうした外需の好調は内需にも波及している。
良好な雇用・所得環境を背景に家計の消費行動
は底堅く、個人消費は同＋4.8％と、前期の同＋
4.7％から小幅に伸びが高まった。生活支援策の

拡大を受けて、政府消費も大幅に増加した。一
方、総固定資本形成は同＋4.6％と、これまでの
高い伸び （前期同＋7.3％） から増勢が鈍化した。
中東情勢の不透明感が、企業の投資計画の実行
延期を招いたと考えられる。
先行きを展望すると、マレーシア経済は拡大
が続くものの、成長ペースは徐々に鈍化する見
通しである。世界的な AI ブームが当面継続し、
外需の堅調が続く一方、個人消費の増勢は徐々に鈍化するとみられる。不透明な中東情勢を背景
としたエネルギー価格の高止まりや、エルニーニョ現象による農業生産の停滞・食料品価格の高
騰がインフレ圧力を高め、家計消費を下押しすると予想される。
■半導体産業の高度化に向けた取り組みが具体化
マレーシア政府は半導体産業の高付加価値化に向けて、4 月以降、IC 設計支援や先端パッケー
ジング投資を柱とする一連の取り組みを公表した（右下表）。こうした取り組みが軌道に乗れば、
従来強みとしてきた半導体製造の後工程（組立・検査）を土台に、上流の設計や先端分野へと産

業の裾野を広げ、中長期的な成長力の向上につながると期待される。もっとも、同地域のベトナ
ムやフィリピンも後工程が中心の半導体産業の改革に乗り出している。域内での投資・人材獲得
競争が激化するなか、マレーシアが競争優位を確保できるかは、高度人材の育成や政策推進のス
ピードにかかっていると言えよう。
研究員 呉 子婧（ wu.zijing@jri.co.jp）

JAPAN RESEARCH INSTITUTE
8
中 国  景気は停滞局面続く
■内需低迷が経済の足かせに
中国景気は、 外需が好調に推移しているものの、
内需の低迷を補いきれない状況が続いている。
輸出（ドル建て）は高い伸びを持続し、8月は
前年同月比＋25.0％と、前月（同＋23.9％）から
小幅加速した。世界的な AI ブームを背景に IT
関連財の輸出が好調だったほか、EV需要の高ま
りを受け、 自動車輸出台数も3カ月連続で過去最
高水準となった。国・地域別では、米中関係の改
善を受け、対米輸出が持ち直した。
一方、内需は低迷が続いている（右上図）。8
月の固定資産投資は同▲10.6％と、 大幅な減少が
続いた。 ハイテク製造業による投資は政策支援へ
の期待を背景に増加したものの、 投資全体を押し
上げるには至らなかった。その他の業種では、価
格競争や生産調整を背景に投資は総じて縮小し
た。不動産開発投資も、市況悪化を背景に大幅減

が続いている。消費も停滞している。買い替え補
助で喚起された需要の反動減が自動車販売で顕
著になったほか、 宝飾品や高級店の販売減も加速
した。
消費者物価は、8 月に同＋0.8％と横ばい圏で
推移した。 原油価格や半導体価格の高騰による影
響はあるものの、 内需停滞によるディスインフレ
圧力の方が上回ったためとみられる。
■政府の成長率目標達成は困難
先行きも、中国経済は内需の停滞が続く見込みである。
固定資産投資は、政府の産業支援策を受けたハイテク製造業の投資拡大や、次世代通信網や物
流網の整備といった「六つのネットワーク」戦略を背景に、インフラ投資が増勢に転じる可能性
がある。もっとも、生産調整などの下押し要因が根強く残るため、本格回復は見込みにくい。
消費も、財を中心に低迷が続くと見込まれる。若年層を中心とした厳しい雇用・所得環境、家
計の根強い節約志向を背景に、消費者マインドの改善は当面期待できそうにない。
こうしたなか、習近平政権は8 月下旬、経済政策に関する論評を発表した（右下表）。それに

よると、供給が強く需要が弱い状態を経済運営上の重要課題と認めつつも、「強い景気刺激策に
過度に依存しない」方針を示した。加えて、月次指標や成長率の変化よりも、経済の質の向上や
リスク解消を重視する姿勢も明確にした。このため政府は、こうした方向性に反するような、即
効性・規模重視の景気刺激策には当分踏み切らない可能性が高い。 これにより、 政府が掲げた2026
年の成長率目標（＋4.5～5.0％）の達成は一段と遠のいたとみられる。
項目 主な指摘事項
・供給が強く、需要が弱い状況（供強需
弱）はなお突出。
・成⻑速度や⽉次指標の変化より質の向上
を重視する傾向に変化。
・強い景気刺激策に、過度に依存しない経
済運営を継続。
・地⽅政府債務や不動産市場などのリスク
解消策に注⼒。
・こうした⽅針は、短期の成⻑率にはマイナス
となるも、⻑期かつ持続的な経済発展には
プラス。
今後の経済
⾒通し
・年後半に、第15次5カ年計画等で確定し
た重点プロジェクトを執⾏すれば、経済は
安定的に運営。

Document 521: インドネシアGDP（26年7-9月期）～成長率は5.47％に加速、製造業とサービス業が牽引

Source PDF: all report pdf/インドネシアGDP（26年7-9月期）～成長率は5.47％に加速、製造業とサービス業が牽引.pdf
Report source: NLI
Date: 2026-11-05
Pages: 3
Status: ok

### Page 1

【結果概要】 
インドネシアの2026年7-9月期実質GDP成長率（速報値）1は前年同期比5.47％となり、前期の
同 5.29％から加速し、Bloomberg調査の市場予想（5.20％）を上回った（図表１）。個人消費が同
5.04％と底堅く推移したほか、政府消費はMBG（無償給食）の関連支出などを背景に同8.95％と高
い伸びを維持したものの、 前期から鈍化した。 総固定資本形成も同6.06％と伸びが鈍化した。 一方、
外需では輸入の伸びが輸出を上回り、純輸出は成長率を下押ししたが、マイナス幅は前期から縮小
した。 
1-9月期の成長率は前年同期比 5.45％となり、政府の 2026年成長率目標である 5.4％を上回っ
た。年末にかけては、政府の追加景気対策が家計消費を下支えするとみられる一方、2027年の成長
加速に向けて民間投資の勢いを強めるかが注目される。

【支出別の詳細】 
支出別にみると、内需は引き続き堅調に推移した。家計最終消費支出は前年同期比5.04％と前期
の同 5.06％からほぼ横ばいとなり、底堅さを維持した（図表２） 。外食需要の拡大に加え、家計の
移動の活発化や公演・スポーツイベントの開催が消費を下支えした。内訳では、外食・宿泊が同
6.59％、交通・通信が同5.83％と比較的高い伸びを維持した一方、食料・飲料は同4.15％、被服・
履物は同3.48％と緩やかな伸びにとどまった。 
政府最終消費支出は同8.95％と前期の同15.97％から減速したものの、引き続き高い伸びとなっ

～成長率は 5.47％に加速、製造業とサービス業が牽引

### Page 2

【産業別の詳細】 
第三次産業（サービス業）は前年同期比7.19％と、前期（同6.22％）から加速し、引き続き成長
の中心となった（図表３） 。内訳をみると、構成割合の大きい卸売・小売業（同7.14％）は国内生
産や輸入の増加に伴う商品流通の拡大に加え、自動車販売の回復などを背景に堅調だった（図表４） 。
宿泊・飲食業は同11.22％と、ホテル稼働率の上昇や外食需要の拡大を受けて高い伸びを維持した。
運輸・倉庫業（同8.03％）も道路・海上輸送の拡大を背景に加速したほか、情報・通信（同7.13％）
はデータ通信やデジタルサービスの利用拡大に支えられて堅調だった。 また、 金融・保険 （同8.47％）
は銀行の金融仲介機能の拡大などを受けて大きく加速した。 
第二次産業は前年同期比5.16％となり、前期（同4.08％）から加速した。このうち、GDP全体の
約２割を占める製造業は同5.87％と、前期の同4.52％から伸びが加速した。内訳をみると、食料・
飲料（同7.09％）がパーム油関連製品の国内外の需要拡大を受けて堅調に伸びたほか、化学・医薬
品（同 8.10％）や基礎金属（同 9.94％）もバイオディーゼル原料や非鉄金属などの生産増加を背
景に加速した。一方、金属製品・コンピューター・電子・光学・電気機器（同2.33％）は前期から
減速し、ゴム・プラスチック製品（同▲7.96％）も引き続き減少した。鉱業・採石業は同0.91％と
前期の同▲1.64％からプラスに転じた。金属鉱石の生産が持ち直した一方、石油・ガスや石炭の生
